**5.** A 71-year-old man presents 90 min after the sudden onset of a right homonymous hemianopia, expressive and receptive dysphasia, and weakness of the right face, arm and leg. He is right-handed, takes no anticoagulants and his glucose is 5.8 mmol/L. CT shows no haemorrhage. According to the Oxfordshire (Bamford) classification, which category fits best?

- A. Lacunar syndrome (LACS)
- B. Partial anterior circulation syndrome (PACS)
- C. Posterior circulation syndrome (POCS)
- D. Total anterior circulation syndrome (TACS)
- E. Transient ischaemic attack

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** TACS requires all three: higher cerebral dysfunction (dysphasia, neglect), homonymous visual field defect and motor/sensory deficit in ≥ 2 of face, arm, leg. PACS = two of the three or isolated cortical deficit; LACS = pure motor/sensory without cortical signs; POCS = brainstem/cerebellar or isolated hemianopia. TACS has the worst prognosis (~40 % 30-day mortality) and usually reflects a proximal MCA/ICA occlusion – a thrombectomy candidate. Ref: Bamford J et al. *Lancet* 1991.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W15-D5 · Status epilepticus

**1.** A 45-year-old man has been having a generalised convulsive seizure for 7 minutes on arrival of the ambulance; no IV access can be obtained. Regarding drug treatment of convulsive status epilepticus:

- A. Intramuscular midazolam 10 mg is an effective first-line alternative to IV lorazepam when venous access is not available. — T / F
- B. After failure of benzodiazepines, levetiracetam, fosphenytoin and valproate have similar efficacy. — T / F
- C. Status epilepticus is termed refractory when seizures continue after a benzodiazepine and one second-line antiseizure drug. — T / F
- D. Rocuronium terminates electrographic seizure activity and facilitates ventilation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.** RAMPART showed IM midazolam (autoinjector) was at least as effective as IV lorazepam pre-hospital. ESETT found levetiracetam 60 mg/kg, fosphenytoin 20 mg PE/kg and valproate 40 mg/kg each stopped seizures in ~50 %. Refractory SE = failure of first- and second-line therapy, requiring anaesthetic agents (midazolam, propofol, thiopental) with EEG monitoring. Neuromuscular blockers abolish the motor signs only – seizures continue electrographically, so EEG is mandatory if they are used. Ref: Silbergleit R et al. *N Engl J Med* 2012; Kapur J et al. *N Engl J Med* 2019; Trinka E et al. *Epilepsia* 2015.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D1 · Analgesia and sedation

**1.** A 63-year-old man with Child–Pugh C alcohol-related cirrhosis is intubated for severe community-acquired pneumonia. He has oliguric AKI (creatinine 310 µmol/L) and is not yet on renal replacement therapy. He needs an opioid infusion for analgesia-based sedation to tolerate the ventilator. Which opioid has pharmacokinetics least affected by his hepatic and renal dysfunction?

- A. Remifentanil
- B. Morphine
- C. Oxycodone
- D. Pethidine (meperidine)
- E. Codeine

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Remifentanil is hydrolysed by non-specific blood and tissue esterases, so its context-sensitive half-time (~3–4 min) is unchanged by liver or kidney failure. Morphine-6-glucuronide (active) and morphine-3-glucuronide accumulate in renal failure; oxycodone depends on hepatic CYP metabolism and renal excretion; norpethidine accumulates and causes seizures; codeine is a prodrug with unpredictable CYP2D6 activation. Note that the remifentanil metabolite (remifentanil acid) accumulates in renal failure but has negligible activity. Ref: Devlin JW et al. *Crit Care Med* 2018 (PADIS); Breen D et al. *Crit Care* 2004.

</details>

**2.** A 48-year-old man on long-term oxycodone for chronic back pain is in ICU after an emergency laparotomy. His thoracic epidural fell out on day 1. Despite escalating IV morphine PCA his pain score remains 8/10, limiting coughing. He is on low-dose noradrenaline, creatinine is rising, and he has a prolonged ileus. Which is the most appropriate additional analgesic strategy?

- A. IV ketorolac 30 mg 8-hourly
- B. IV clonidine infusion
- C. Gabapentin via nasogastric tube
- D. Double the morphine PCA bolus dose
- E. Low-dose IV ketamine infusion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.** Low-dose ketamine (e.g. 0.1–0.3 mg/kg/h) is an effective opioid-sparing adjunct, especially in opioid-tolerant patients and after major abdominal surgery; PADIS suggests it in post-surgical ICU patients. NSAIDs are inadvisable with AKI and vasopressor need; clonidine worsens hypotension; gabapentinoids require enteral absorption (ileus) and act slowly; further opioid escalation worsens ileus without solving tolerance. Ref: Devlin JW et al. *Crit Care Med* 2018 (PADIS); Brinck ECV et al. *Cochrane Database Syst Rev* 2018.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D2 · Delirium

**1.** A 72-year-old woman is on day 5 of mechanical ventilation for pneumonia. Sedation has been stopped for a spontaneous breathing trial, but she is RASS +3, CAM-ICU positive and repeatedly tries to pull out her tube despite reorientation, family presence and pain control. She has no history of alcohol use. QTc is 520 ms. Which drug is the most appropriate choice?

- A. Lorazepam IV bolus
- B. Dexmedetomidine infusion
- C. Haloperidol 5 mg IV 6-hourly
- D. Quetiapine via nasogastric tube
- E. Propofol infusion to RASS −4

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** For agitated delirium that prevents weaning/extubation, PADIS suggests dexmedetomidine; DahLIA showed more ventilator-free hours with dexmedetomidine than placebo in agitated delirium. With QTc 520 ms, haloperidol and quetiapine add arrhythmic risk, and neither shortens delirium (MIND-USA, AID-ICU). Benzodiazepines are a risk factor for delirium; deep propofol sedation delays weaning. Ref: Reade MC et al. *JAMA* 2016 (DahLIA); Devlin JW et al. *Crit Care Med* 2018 (PADIS); Andersen-Ranberg NC et al. *N Engl J Med* 2022.

</details>

**2.** A surgical ICU wants to reduce delirium among its elderly ventilated patients. Regarding delirium prevention:

- A. Higher compliance with the ABCDEF bundle is associated with less delirium and coma. — T / F
- B. Prophylactic low-dose haloperidol in high-risk patients reduced 28-day mortality in the REDUCE trial. — T / F
- C. Non-pharmacological sleep promotion (earplugs, eye masks, noise reduction) is suggested by PADIS. — T / F
- D. Rivastigmine added to haloperidol shortens delirium duration. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.** In >15 000 patients, ABCDEF bundle compliance was associated with less delirium, coma, restraint use and mortality. REDUCE (haloperidol 1–2 mg 8-hourly in 1789 patients) showed no effect on 28-day survival or delirium. PADIS suggests a multicomponent sleep-promoting strategy. The rivastigmine trial was stopped early because of higher mortality and longer delirium. Ref: Pun BT et al. *Crit Care Med* 2019; van den Boogaard M et al. *JAMA* 2018; van Eijk MM et al. *Lancet* 2010.

</details>

**3.** A nurse is assessing a 66-year-old ventilated patient with the CAM-ICU. Regarding this tool:

- A. A patient with a RASS of −4 cannot be assessed and is considered comatose. — T / F
- B. Inattention (feature 2) is tested with the Mini-Mental State Examination. — T / F
- C. A positive result requires all four features to be present. — T / F
- D. A known diagnosis of dementia precludes the use of CAM-ICU. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.** Assessment starts with the RASS: at −4/−5 the patient is comatose and is reassessed later. Inattention is tested with the letters test (squeeze on "A") or pictures. CAM-ICU is positive with features 1 (acute change/fluctuation) + 2 (inattention) + either 3 (altered level of consciousness, RASS ≠ 0) or 4 (disorganised thinking). It remains valid in patients with dementia (it detects acute change from baseline). Ref: Ely EW et al. *JAMA* 2001; Devlin JW et al. *Crit Care Med* 2018.

</details>

**4.** A 57-year-old man has a 6-day episode of ICU delirium after septic shock. Regarding the epidemiology and consequences of ICU delirium:

- A. Longer duration of delirium is independently associated with worse long-term cognitive impairment. — T / F
- B. Hyperactive delirium is the most common subtype in ventilated patients. — T / F
- C. An Intensive Care Delirium Screening Checklist (ICDSC) score ≥ 4 indicates delirium. — T / F
- D. Haloperidol or ziprasidone shortened delirium duration compared with placebo in MIND-USA. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.** BRAIN-ICU showed delirium duration predicted global cognition at 3 and 12 months. Hypoactive and mixed forms predominate and are often missed. ICDSC (8 items) ≥ 4 indicates delirium. MIND-USA found no difference in days alive without delirium or coma with haloperidol or ziprasidone versus placebo. Ref: Pandharipande PP et al. *N Engl J Med* 2013; Girard TD et al. *N Engl J Med* 2018; Bergeron N et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2001.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D3 · Neuromuscular disorders

**1.** A 26-year-old student develops progressive limb weakness 10 days after a diarrhoeal illness. Regarding the epidemiology and immunology of Guillain–Barré syndrome (GBS):

- A. About two-thirds of patients report an infection in the weeks before onset. — T / F
- B. Anti-GQ1b antibodies are characteristic of the pure motor axonal (AMAN) variant. — T / F
- C. The annual incidence is about 20 per 100 000 population. — T / F
- D. Weakness typically continues to progress for more than 8 weeks before reaching its nadir. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.** Around two-thirds have a preceding respiratory or gastrointestinal infection (Campylobacter jejuni most often; also CMV, EBV, Mycoplasma, Zika, SARS-CoV-2). Anti-GQ1b is associated with Miller Fisher syndrome and Bickerstaff encephalitis; AMAN is associated with anti-GM1/GD1a. Incidence is ~1–2 per 100 000/year. Nadir is reached within 4 weeks (usually 2); progression > 8 weeks suggests CIDP. Ref: Shahrizaila N et al. *Lancet* 2021; Leonhard SE et al. *Nat Rev Neurol* 2019.

</details>

**2.** Several patients with neuromuscular disease need emergency intubation. Regarding the use of neuromuscular blocking drugs:

- A. In Duchenne muscular dystrophy, suxamethonium can cause hyperkalaemic cardiac arrest. — T / F
- B. In myotonic dystrophy, suxamethonium can cause generalised myotonic contracture that makes ventilation difficult. — T / F
- C. Patients with myasthenia gravis are less sensitive than normal to non-depolarising neuromuscular blockers. — T / F
- D. Sugammadex can reverse rocuronium-induced block in myasthenia gravis. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.** Dystrophic and denervated muscle can release large amounts of potassium (rhabdomyolysis/upregulated receptors) after suxamethonium. Myotonic patients may develop sustained contracture. Myasthenic patients are resistant to suxamethonium but very sensitive to non-depolarising agents – use reduced doses with quantitative monitoring. Sugammadex encapsulates rocuronium independently of acetylcholinesterase and has been used successfully in MG. Ref: Martyn JAJ, Richtsfeld M. *Anesthesiology* 2006; Blichfeldt-Lauridsen L, Hansen BD. *Acta Anaesthesiol Scand* 2012.

</details>

**3.** A 61-year-old woman with generalised myasthenia gravis is admitted to ICU with pneumonia and hypomagnesaemia. Which of the following drugs are recognised to precipitate or worsen myasthenic weakness?

- A. Intravenous magnesium sulphate — T / F
- B. Azithromycin — T / F
- C. Ceftriaxone — T / F
- D. Paracetamol — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.** Magnesium reduces presynaptic acetylcholine release and can provoke crisis. Macrolides, fluoroquinolones and aminoglycosides impair neuromuscular transmission (FDA warnings for macrolides and quinolones). Others include beta-blockers, immune checkpoint inhibitors, quinine and botulinum toxin. Third-generation cephalosporins and paracetamol are considered safe choices. Ref: Sheikh S et al. *Muscle Nerve* 2021; Narayanaswami P et al. *Neurology* 2021.

</details>

**4.** A 38-year-old man with 4 days of ascending weakness and areflexia is in a high-dependency unit receiving IVIg. He now has a weak cough, difficulty swallowing saliva and bifacial weakness. SpO2 is 97 % on air and PaCO2 5.1 kPa. Bedside vital capacity has fallen from 32 to 17 ml/kg over 12 h, and maximal inspiratory pressure is −25 cmH2O. Which is the most appropriate next step?

- A. Elective endotracheal intubation in the ICU
- B. Non-invasive ventilation via a face mask
- C. Continue observation until PaCO2 rises above 6.5 kPa
- D. Switch from IVIg to plasma exchange and repeat vital capacity in 6 h
- E. Start pyridostigmine

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** The "20/30/40 rule" (VC < 20 ml/kg, MIP weaker than −30 cmH2O, MEP < 40 cmH2O), rapid decline and bulbar dysfunction predict respiratory failure; intubation should be elective before hypoxaemia or hypercapnia, which are late signs. NIV is unsafe with bulbar weakness and aspiration risk. Changing immunotherapy does not address the imminent airway problem (and PE after IVIg is not beneficial); pyridostigmine has no role in GBS. Ref: Lawn ND et al. *Arch Neurol* 2001; Shahrizaila N et al. *Lancet* 2021.

</details>

**5.** A 31-year-old woman reports 3 weeks of drooping eyelids and double vision that are worse in the evening, and tiredness of her jaw towards the end of meals. Neck flexion weakens after repeated testing. Reflexes, sensation and CK are normal. CT chest shows an anterior mediastinal mass. Which is the most likely diagnosis?

- A. Lambert–Eaton myasthenic syndrome
- B. Polymyositis
- C. Myasthenia gravis
- D. Miller Fisher syndrome
- E. Myotonic dystrophy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Fatigable ocular, bulbar and proximal weakness without sensory signs, normal reflexes and CK, plus a thymic mass (thymoma or hyperplasia), is typical of acetylcholine-receptor antibody myasthenia gravis. LEMS has proximal leg weakness, areflexia that improves after exercise, autonomic features and is associated with small-cell lung cancer. Polymyositis raises CK; Miller Fisher gives ophthalmoplegia, ataxia and areflexia; myotonic dystrophy has myotonia and distal weakness. Ref: Gilhus NE. *N Engl J Med* 2016.

</details>

**6.** A 44-year-old man with suspected GBS has a lumbar puncture on day 3 of symptoms. Regarding CSF findings:

- A. CSF protein may still be normal in the first week of illness. — T / F
- B. A CSF white cell count > 50 cells/µl should prompt a search for an alternative diagnosis. — T / F
- C. A low CSF glucose is typical of GBS. — T / F
- D. Albuminocytological dissociation is specific to GBS. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.** Protein is raised in only ~50 % in the first week (~90 % by week 3), so a normal early result does not exclude GBS. Mild pleocytosis (5–50/µl) is compatible, but > 50/µl suggests infection (HIV, Lyme, CMV polyradiculitis), leptomeningeal malignancy or sarcoid. Glucose is normal. Raised protein with normal cells is also seen in CIDP, spinal block, diabetes and other conditions. Ref: Leonhard SE et al. *Nat Rev Neurol* 2019; Fokke C et al. *Brain* 2014.

</details>

**7.** A 29-year-old man who injects black-tar heroin into his thigh presents with 2 days of double vision, slurred speech and difficulty swallowing. He is alert and afebrile, has bilateral ptosis, poorly reactive dilated pupils, a dry mouth and symmetrical weakness of the neck and arms; sensation is normal. There is an indurated abscess on his thigh. In addition to supportive care and wound debridement, which treatment is most important?

- A. Intravenous immunoglobulin
- B. Plasma exchange
- C. Pyridostigmine
- D. Benzylpenicillin alone
- E. Botulinum antitoxin

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.** Descending symmetrical paralysis starting with cranial nerves, autonomic signs (dilated pupils, dry mouth), normal sensation and preserved consciousness in an injecting drug user = wound botulism. Antitoxin should be given as early as possible on clinical suspicion without waiting for confirmation, because it only neutralises circulating toxin. Antibiotics are given after antitoxin with debridement; IVIg/plasma exchange are GBS treatments; pyridostigmine is ineffective. Ref: Rao AK et al. *MMWR Recomm Rep* 2021 (CDC botulism guidelines).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D4 · Brain death and confirmation of death

**1.** A 53-year-old man with a massive intracerebral haemorrhage is being tested for death by neurological criteria according to the UK Code of Practice. Regarding the brainstem reflex tests:

- A. The vestibulo-ocular reflex is tested with at least 50 ml of ice-cold water instilled into each external auditory meatus over 1 minute. — T / F
- B. Absent gag and cough reflexes indicate loss of glossopharyngeal and vagal (medullary) function. — T / F
- C. The pupils need not be dilated, but must be fixed and unresponsive to bright light. — T / F
- D. Preserved deep tendon reflexes in the legs preclude the diagnosis. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.** The tests assess the cranial-nerve reflex arcs: pupils (II/III), corneal (V/VII), vestibulo-ocular (VIII/III/VI, ≥ 50 ml ice-cold water over 1 min with tympanic membranes visible), motor response in cranial nerve distribution to central pain, gag and cough (IX/X), then apnoea. Mid-sized fixed pupils are compatible. Spinal reflexes – tendon reflexes, triple flexion, even Lazarus sign – may persist and do not preclude diagnosis. Ref: Academy of Medical Royal Colleges. *Code of Practice for the Diagnosis and Confirmation of Death* 2008 (rev. 2025); Greer DM et al. *JAMA* 2020 (World Brain Death Project).

</details>

**2.** A 30-year-old woman with hypoxic brain injury after cardiac arrest is being considered for brainstem death testing in the UK. Which of the following are correct preconditions or apnoea-test requirements?

- A. Before the apnoea test PaCO2 should be ≥ 6.0 kPa with pH < 7.40, and it must rise by > 0.5 kPa with no respiratory effort. — T / F
- B. Blood glucose should be between 3.0 and 20 mmol/L. — T / F
- C. Serum sodium must be between 135 and 145 mmol/L. — T / F
- D. Core temperature must be ≥ 36 °C. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.** UK guidance requires reversible causes to be excluded: core temperature > 34 °C, sodium 115–160 mmol/L, potassium > 2 mmol/L, phosphate and magnesium 0.5–3.0 mmol/L, glucose 3.0–20 mmol/L, and no residual sedative or neuromuscular blocking effect. In the apnoea test, starting PaCO2 ≥ 6.0 kPa (pH < 7.40) and a rise ≥ 0.5 kPa without respiratory effort over 5 min confirm apnoea (higher starting values in chronic CO2 retainers). Ref: Academy of Medical Royal Colleges. *Code of Practice for the Diagnosis and Confirmation of Death* 2008 (rev. 2025); Greer DM et al. *JAMA* 2020.

</details>

**3.** An 84-year-old man with metastatic cancer, on a palliative pathway in a monitored ward bed, has a cardiorespiratory arrest. Regarding confirmation of death after circulatory arrest (UK practice):

- A. Death should be confirmed only after a minimum of 5 minutes of observed continuous cardiorespiratory arrest. — T / F
- B. An EEG must be performed before death after circulatory arrest is confirmed. — T / F
- C. If spontaneous cardiac activity returns during the observation period, the 5 minutes continue from where they were interrupted. — T / F
- D. Ancillary imaging (e.g. CT angiography) is mandatory in all UK diagnoses of death by neurological criteria. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.** After 5 min of continuous asystole (ECG, absent arterial pulsatility or echo), absence of pupillary and corneal reflexes and of motor response to supraorbital pressure is confirmed and the time recorded. Any return of cardiac or respiratory activity restarts a fresh 5-minute observation. EEG is not part of confirmation. In the UK, ancillary tests are only needed when the clinical tests cannot be completed or confounders cannot be excluded (e.g. high cervical cord injury, extensive facial trauma). Ref: Academy of Medical Royal Colleges. *Code of Practice for the Diagnosis and Confirmation of Death* 2008 (rev. 2025).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W16-D5 · Intracranial pressure monitoring

**1.** A 35-year-old man with severe TBI has ICP monitoring. Regarding ICP monitoring devices and derived indices:

- A. Intraparenchymal microtransducers cannot be recalibrated in situ and may drift over time. — T / F
- B. A ventricular catheter allows both ICP measurement and therapeutic CSF drainage. — T / F
- C. A positive pressure-reactivity index (PRx) indicates intact cerebral autoregulation. — T / F
- D. Lundberg B waves are rhythmic oscillations at 0.5–2 per minute linked to vasomotor activity. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.** Parenchymal sensors are zeroed only before insertion and may drift by a few mmHg over days; an EVD is the reference standard and can drain CSF (but not measure accurately while open). PRx is a moving correlation between slow waves of MAP and ICP: negative/near-zero values indicate preserved reactivity; positive values (> 0.2–0.3) indicate impaired autoregulation and are associated with worse outcome and are used to estimate CPPopt. B waves reflect vasogenic oscillations and are seen with reduced compensatory reserve. Ref: Le Roux P et al. *Neurocrit Care* 2014; Czosnyka M, Pickard JD. *J Neurol Neurosurg Psychiatry* 2004.

</details>

**2.** A 19-year-old woman with diffuse brain swelling has a continuous ICP trace displayed. Regarding the ICP waveform:

- A. The percussion wave (P1) reflects arterial pulsation transmitted to the CSF. — T / F
- B. The dicrotic wave (P3) follows the dicrotic notch. — T / F
- C. An increasing ICP pulse amplitude as mean ICP rises (RAP approaching +1) indicates reduced compensatory reserve. — T / F
- D. Lundberg A (plateau) waves are sustained rises to 50–100 mmHg lasting several minutes and are always pathological. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.** P1 = arterial pulsation (choroid plexus), P2 = tidal wave reflecting brain compliance (P2 > P1 when compliance is poor), P3 = dicrotic wave after the notch. RAP (correlation between pulse amplitude and mean ICP) near 0 means good reserve, near +1 means exhausted reserve, and falling values at very high ICP signal terminal vasomotor collapse. Plateau waves (5–20 min) occur with intact autoregulation but poor compliance and indicate risk of ischaemia. Ref: Czosnyka M, Pickard JD. *J Neurol Neurosurg Psychiatry* 2004; Hawryluk GWJ et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2019.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W17-D1 · Spinal cord injury

**1.** A 40-year-old man falls from a ladder and sustains a C6 fracture-dislocation. On day 3 the ISNCSCI examination shows a neurological level of C6, preserved light touch and pin-prick at S4–5, voluntary anal contraction, and power of grade 3–4/5 in most key muscles below the level of injury. Which ASIA Impairment Scale (AIS) grade is correct?

- A. AIS A
- B. AIS B
- C. AIS C
- D. AIS D
- E. AIS E

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** AIS A = complete (no sensory or motor function in S4–5); B = sensory but no motor function preserved below the level (including sacral segments); C = motor function preserved below the level with more than half of key muscles below grade 3; D = motor incomplete with at least half of key muscles below the level grade ≥ 3; E = normal. Voluntary anal contraction makes him motor incomplete, and most key muscles ≥ 3 makes him AIS D. Ref: Kirshblum SC et al. *J Spinal Cord Med* 2011; Rupp R et al. *Top Spinal Cord Inj Rehabil* 2021.

</details>

**2.** A 22-year-old man has a complete C5 injury after a diving accident. In the emergency department his HR is 44/min, BP 78/40 mmHg, peripheries are warm and he is breathing with paradoxical abdominal movement. A CT shows a C5 burst fracture with no other obvious injuries, and his vital capacity is 1.4 L. All of the following are appropriate in his early management EXCEPT:

- A. Exclude occult haemorrhage with focused ultrasound and CT before attributing hypotension to the cord injury
- B. Give atropine for symptomatic bradycardia, particularly during tracheal suctioning
- C. Use suxamethonium for re-intubation on day 10 because of its rapid onset
- D. Use a vasopressor after adequate volume to maintain MAP at about 75–80 mmHg or higher for 3–7 days
- E. Monitor vital capacity closely, anticipating ventilatory failure

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** From about 72 h after denervation, extrajunctional acetylcholine receptors proliferate and suxamethonium can cause life-threatening hyperkalaemia; it should be avoided for ~6–9 months. Neurogenic shock (bradycardia, vasodilatation) must be distinguished from haemorrhage. Vagal reflexes are unopposed, so suction may cause asystole – atropine should be available. The 2024 AO Spine/Praxis guideline suggests MAP ≥ 75–80 (upper 90–95) mmHg for 3–7 days. VC monitoring is key since C3–5 lesions compromise the diaphragm. Ref: Kwon BK et al. *Global Spine J* 2024; Martyn JAJ, Richtsfeld M. *Anesthesiology* 2006.

</details>

**3.** A 36-year-old woman has a complete C4 spinal cord injury after a road traffic collision. Regarding her ongoing management:

- A. Lesions at or above C3–C5 commonly require early ventilatory support because of diaphragmatic weakness. — T / F
- B. An abdominal binder can improve vital capacity in a tetraplegic patient sitting upright. — T / F
- C. Autonomic dysreflexia typically complicates lesions below T10. — T / F
- D. Early surgical decompression (< 24 h) is associated with better neurological recovery. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.** The phrenic nerve (C3–5) is affected, and fatigue often causes deterioration over the first days. In tetraplegia, VC is better supine; when sitting, abdominal contents drop and the diaphragm flattens – a binder restores its position. Autonomic dysreflexia (paroxysmal hypertension with reflex bradycardia, often from bladder or bowel distension) occurs with lesions at or above T6. STASCIS and later meta-analyses support decompression within 24 h. Ref: Fehlings MG et al. *PLoS One* 2012 (STASCIS); Fehlings MG et al. *Global Spine J* 2017.

</details>

**4.** A 55-year-old man is ventilated on day 3 after severe TBI and is expected to remain obtunded for several more days. On admission he had a multidetector CT of the cervical spine, reported by a consultant radiologist as normal. His rigid collar is making it hard to keep ICP under control and he has an early occipital pressure sore. What is the most appropriate management of his cervical spine?

- A. Keep the collar until he can be assessed clinically
- B. Remove the collar on the basis of the normal CT
- C. Obtain MRI of the cervical spine before removing the collar
- D. Perform dynamic flexion-extension fluoroscopy
- E. Replace the collar with sandbags and tape

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** In obtunded adult blunt trauma patients, a negative high-quality CT reported by an experienced radiologist has a very high negative predictive value for unstable injury; EAST recommends collar removal on CT alone. Prolonged collars raise ICP (jugular venous compression), cause pressure ulcers, and complicate airway care and line access. MRI rarely detects injuries that change management, and dynamic fluoroscopy is unsafe and not recommended. Ref: Patel MB et al. *J Trauma Acute Care Surg* 2015 (EAST).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W17-D2 · ICU-acquired weakness

**1.** A 64-year-old woman cannot lift her arms off the bed after 3 weeks of ventilation for septic shock. Regarding ICU-acquired weakness (ICUAW):

- A. Hyperglycaemia is a modifiable risk factor. — T / F
- B. Early supplemental parenteral nutrition reduces the incidence of ICUAW. — T / F
- C. A Medical Research Council (MRC) sum score < 48/60 defines ICUAW. — T / F
- D. Handgrip dynamometry can be used as a quick bedside screening test in cooperative patients. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – T.** Hyperglycaemia, sepsis/multiorgan failure, immobility and prolonged ventilation are risk factors; intensive insulin reduced electrophysiological CIP/CIM in the Leuven trials (at a hypoglycaemia cost). In EPaNIC, late PN (withholding PN for a week) reduced weakness and accelerated recovery compared with early PN. MRC-SS < 48 in a cooperative patient defines ICUAW; handgrip dynamometry (< 11 kg men, < 7 kg women) is a quick screening tool. Ref: Hermans G et al. *Lancet Respir Med* 2013; Vanhorebeek I et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2020.

</details>

**2.** A 60-year-old man received high-dose hydrocortisone and 48 h of cisatracurium for septic shock with severe ARDS. On day 18 he fails repeated weaning trials and has flaccid, symmetrical limb weakness. Neurophysiology is requested to distinguish critical illness polyneuropathy (CIP) from critical illness myopathy (CIM). Which finding best supports CIP rather than CIM?

- A. Reduced compound muscle action potential (CMAP) amplitudes
- B. Normal motor conduction velocities
- C. Reduced sensory nerve action potential (SNAP) amplitudes
- D. Fibrillation potentials on needle EMG
- E. Decrement > 10 % on repetitive nerve stimulation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Both CIP and CIM reduce CMAP amplitudes with relatively preserved conduction velocities, and fibrillation potentials can occur in both. The discriminating feature is sensory involvement: SNAP amplitudes are reduced in CIP (axonal sensorimotor neuropathy) but normal in pure CIM. In CIM, CMAPs are prolonged and direct muscle stimulation shows reduced muscle excitability. A decrement on repetitive stimulation suggests a junctional disorder such as myasthenia or persisting neuromuscular block. Ref: Latronico N, Bolton CF. *Lancet Neurol* 2011; Vanhorebeek I et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W17-D3 · Alcohol-related neurological emergencies

**1.** A 52-year-old man with long-standing alcohol dependence and weight loss is admitted with pneumonia. On day 2 he is confused, has a broad-based unsteady gait, horizontal nystagmus and bilateral lateral rectus weakness. He is not tremulous, HR 88/min, glucose 6.2 mmol/L, Na 136 mmol/L. Which is the most appropriate immediate treatment?

- A. Oral thiamine 100 mg daily
- B. 50 ml of 50 % glucose IV
- C. High-dose intravenous thiamine (e.g. 500 mg three times daily)
- D. Haloperidol 2.5 mg IV
- E. Lorazepam 2 mg IV followed by a reducing regimen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Confusion, ataxia and ophthalmoplegia (the full triad is present in only ~10–20 %) in a malnourished drinker = Wernicke's encephalopathy, a medical emergency treated with high-dose parenteral thiamine (e.g. 200–500 mg three times daily for 3–5 days). Oral thiamine is poorly absorbed in alcohol users. He is normoglycaemic, so glucose is not needed – but in hypoglycaemic patients glucose must never be delayed for thiamine; give both together. There are no signs of withdrawal requiring benzodiazepines. Ref: Galvin R et al. *Eur J Neurol* 2010 (EFNS guideline); Sechi G, Serra A. *Lancet Neurol* 2007.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W18-D1 · Acute kidney injury

**1.** A 67-year-old man is admitted to ICU after emergency aortic surgery and is at high risk of AKI. Regarding measures to prevent or limit AKI:

- A. In critically ill adults, balanced crystalloids reduced major adverse kidney events at 30 days compared with saline in a large pragmatic trial. — T / F
- B. Fenoldopam is recommended to prevent AKI after major surgery. — T / F
- C. Furosemide to convert oliguric to non-oliguric AKI improves renal recovery. — T / F
- D. Avoiding hyperglycaemia is part of KDIGO-based AKI prevention bundles. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.** SMART (15 802 patients) showed fewer MAKE30 events with balanced crystalloids (absolute difference ~1 %). KDIGO recommends against fenoldopam, low-dose dopamine and diuretics to prevent or treat AKI (except for volume overload). The KDIGO "bundle" – avoid nephrotoxins, optimise volume and perfusion, monitor creatinine and urine output, avoid hyperglycaemia and radiocontrast where possible – reduced AKI after cardiac surgery in PrevAKI. Ref: Semler MW et al. *N Engl J Med* 2018; KDIGO. *Kidney Int Suppl* 2012; Meersch M et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2017.

</details>

**2.** A 58-year-old woman's creatinine rises from 80 to 150 µmol/L over 36 h after septic shock. Regarding AKI staging and biomarkers:

- A. KDIGO stage 3 is defined by a creatinine 2.0–2.9 times baseline. — T / F
- B. Urinary TIMP-2·IGFBP7 is a marker of glomerular filtration rate. — T / F
- C. KDIGO stage 1 requires a creatinine rise of at least 44 µmol/L within 48 h. — T / F
- D. Cystatin C is more influenced by muscle mass than creatinine. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – F, C – F, D – F.** Stage 3 = creatinine ≥ 3.0 × baseline, ≥ 354 µmol/L, initiation of RRT or UO < 0.3 ml/kg/h for ≥ 24 h or anuria ≥ 12 h (2.0–2.9 × is stage 2). TIMP-2·IGFBP7 is a tubular cell-cycle-arrest (stress/damage) marker, not a filtration marker. Stage 1 = rise ≥ 26.5 µmol/L (0.3 mg/dl) within 48 h or 1.5–1.9 × baseline within 7 days, or UO < 0.5 ml/kg/h for 6–12 h. Cystatin C is less dependent on muscle mass than creatinine (useful in sarcopenia). This patient's 1.9-fold rise is stage 1. Ref: KDIGO. *Kidney Int Suppl* 2012; Kashani K et al. *Crit Care* 2013; Ostermann M et al. *JAMA Netw Open* 2020.

</details>

**3.** A 79-year-old woman has had profuse diarrhoea for 4 days while continuing her ramipril. Creatinine is 245 µmol/L (baseline 85), urea 28 mmol/L. Urine output is 0.3 ml/kg/h. Urine sodium is 8 mmol/L, fractional excretion of sodium 0.4 %, urine osmolality 640 mOsm/kg; microscopy shows only a few hyaline casts. She has not received diuretics. Which is the most likely cause of her AKI?

- A. Acute tubular necrosis
- B. Acute interstitial nephritis
- C. Rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis
- D. Post-renal obstruction
- E. Prerenal (haemodynamic) AKI

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.** Avid sodium and water retention (UNa < 20 mmol/L, FeNa < 1 %, urine osmolality > 500 mOsm/kg) with a bland sediment and high urea:creatinine ratio indicates preserved tubular function – prerenal AKI from volume depletion compounded by ACE inhibition. ATN typically shows FeNa > 2 %, isosthenuric urine and muddy brown granular/epithelial casts; AIN white cell casts; glomerulonephritis red cell casts and proteinuria. (After diuretics, FeUrea < 35 % is more reliable.) These indices have limited accuracy in sepsis and CKD. Ref: Kellum JA et al. *Nat Rev Dis Primers* 2021; KDIGO. *Kidney Int Suppl* 2012.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W18-D3 · Renal replacement therapy

**1.** A 61-year-old man on CVVHDF has had three filters clot in 24 h using no anticoagulation. Regarding CRRT circuit anticoagulation:

- A. Regional citrate anticoagulation prolongs filter lifespan compared with systemic heparin. — T / F
- B. A total-to-ionised calcium ratio > 2.5 suggests citrate accumulation. — T / F
- C. Citrate is absolutely contraindicated in any degree of liver dysfunction. — T / F
- D. Citrate anticoagulates the circuit mainly by chelating ionised calcium. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.** In RICH (2020), regional citrate extended filter life (~47 vs 26 h) with fewer bleeding complications than systemic heparin; KDIGO recommends citrate first-line if no contraindication. Citrate chelates ionised calcium (a cofactor of the clotting cascade), keeping circuit iCa ~0.25–0.35 mmol/L; calcium is reinfused systemically. Accumulation (impaired hepatic metabolism, poor perfusion) causes a rising total/ionised Ca ratio (> 2.5), falling systemic iCa and high-anion-gap acidosis. Liver dysfunction is a relative caution, not an absolute contraindication – citrate is used with close monitoring. Ref: Zarbock A et al. *JAMA* 2020 (RICH); KDIGO. *Kidney Int Suppl* 2012.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W18-D4 · Rhabdomyolysis and contrast-associated AKI

**1.** A 33-year-old man develops rhabdomyolysis (CK 85 000 U/L) after lying on the floor for 10 hours following a drug binge. Regarding treatment:

- A. Randomised trials show that sodium bicarbonate reduces the need for RRT in rhabdomyolysis. — T / F
- B. The McMahon score (age, sex, creatinine, calcium, phosphate, bicarbonate, CK, aetiology) predicts the risk of RRT or death. — T / F
- C. Hyperkalaemia is a common early life-threatening complication. — T / F
- D. Mannitol is recommended routinely once urine output is established. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – F, B – T, C – T, D – F.** Early, generous isotonic crystalloid to a high urine output is the mainstay; no RCT supports bicarbonate or mannitol, and they cause hypocalcaemia/alkalosis or osmotic problems. The McMahon score (≥ 6 identifies high risk) outperforms CK alone in predicting RRT or death. Potassium released from damaged muscle can cause early arrhythmias and must be monitored closely. Ref: Bosch X et al. *N Engl J Med* 2009; McMahon GM et al. *JAMA Intern Med* 2013.

</details>

**2.** A 35-year-old man has been trapped for 6 hours under a collapsed concrete wall after an earthquake, with both legs crushed. Rescuers expect to free him in about 30 minutes. He is conscious, HR 118/min, BP 105/60 mmHg, and paramedics have obtained IV access in his arm. There is no urine output to assess. Which measure is the most important to start before extrication?

- A. Apply tourniquets to both thighs and release them after extrication
- B. Intravenous isotonic crystalloid at about 1 L/h
- C. Intravenous furosemide 40 mg
- D. Intravenous mannitol 1 g/kg
- E. Oral potassium-free rehydration solution only

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** In crush injury, release of compression causes sudden hypovolaemia, hyperkalaemia and myoglobin release. IV isotonic fluid (e.g. saline ~1 L/h, 10–15 ml/kg/h) should start before extrication and continue afterwards (titrated to urine output and volume tolerance), which reduces crush-related AKI and cardiac arrest. Tourniquets are only for uncontrolled haemorrhage; diuretics and mannitol are not first-line; oral fluid alone is insufficient. Ref: Sever MS, Vanholder R. *Nephrol Dial Transplant* 2012 (Renal Disaster Relief Task Force recommendations); Bosch X et al. *N Engl J Med* 2009.

</details>

**3.** A 42-year-old woman on simvastatin develops myalgia and dark urine a week after starting gemfibrozil. Regarding the pathophysiology and causes of rhabdomyolysis:

- A. Myoglobin causes renal injury through tubular obstruction, direct haem-iron toxicity and renal vasoconstriction. — T / F
- B. Statin–fibrate (especially gemfibrozil) combinations increase the risk of rhabdomyolysis. — T / F
- C. Malignant hyperthermia and neuroleptic malignant syndrome can cause rhabdomyolysis. — T / F
- D. Myoglobin (~17 kDa) is freely filtered at the glomerulus. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – T.** Myoglobin is small and freely filtered; in acidic, concentrated urine it precipitates with Tamm–Horsfall protein to form casts, while haem iron drives lipid peroxidation and scavenges NO, causing vasoconstriction. Gemfibrozil inhibits statin glucuronidation and raises statin levels. Hypermetabolic states (MH, NMS, serotonin syndrome, exertional heat stroke), trauma, ischaemia, drugs, toxins and infections are common causes. Ref: Bosch X et al. *N Engl J Med* 2009; Petejova N, Martinek A. *Crit Care* 2014.

</details>

**4.** A 70-year-old man with diabetes and stage 3b CKD (eGFR 35 ml/min/1.73 m²) needs a contrast-enhanced CT angiogram for suspected mesenteric ischaemia. He takes metformin and ramipril, is haemodynamically stable and clinically euvolaemic, and the radiologist plans to use iso-osmolar contrast. Which is the most appropriate measure to reduce his risk of contrast-associated AKI?

- A. Oral N-acetylcysteine 1200 mg twice daily
- B. Intravenous sodium bicarbonate instead of saline
- C. Prophylactic haemofiltration around the scan
- D. Peri-procedural IV isotonic crystalloid and minimum necessary dose of iso- or low-osmolar contrast
- E. Defer the CT until renal function improves

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** PRESERVE (2018) showed no benefit of IV bicarbonate over saline or of NAC over placebo. Current ACR/NKF consensus: the risk of contrast-associated AKI with modern agents at eGFR ≥ 30 is low; prophylactic isotonic fluid is reasonable for eGFR < 30 or high-risk patients, and a medically indicated urgent scan should never be withheld. Prophylactic haemofiltration is not recommended. Ref: Weisbord SD et al. *N Engl J Med* 2018 (PRESERVE); Davenport MS et al. *Radiology* 2020.

</details>

**5.** A 28-year-old soldier collapses during a hot-weather march. Twelve hours later his CK is 120 000 U/L and creatinine 260 µmol/L (normal at enlistment). His core temperature was 41.2 °C at collapse and urine is dark brown, dipstick strongly positive for blood. All of the following laboratory findings are typical of his established rhabdomyolysis-associated AKI EXCEPT:

- A. Hyperphosphataemia
- B. Hyperuricaemia
- C. High-anion-gap metabolic acidosis
- D. Hypokalaemia
- E. Creatinine rising disproportionately to urea

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Muscle necrosis releases potassium, phosphate, purines (→ uric acid), organic acids and creatine (→ creatinine), so hyperkalaemia, hyperphosphataemia, hyperuricaemia, high-anion-gap acidosis and a low urea:creatinine ratio are typical. Calcium falls early (deposition in damaged muscle), with rebound hypercalcaemia in the recovery phase. Ref: Bosch X et al. *N Engl J Med* 2009.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W18-D5 · Timing of renal replacement therapy

**1.** A 30-year-old man had a fasciotomy for compartment syndrome 24 h ago and now has rhabdomyolysis (CK 80 000 U/L). After 6 L of crystalloid, urine output is 0.4 ml/kg/h, creatinine 280 µmol/L, K 5.6 mmol/L without ECG changes, bicarbonate 19 mmol/L, pH 7.31. He has no pulmonary oedema and is on 28 % oxygen. What is the most appropriate management?

- A. Start CRRT now with a high cut-off filter to clear myoglobin
- B. Start intermittent haemodialysis now to prevent hyperkalaemia
- C. Continue fluid titrated to volume status, treat potassium medically and monitor closely for urgent RRT indications
- D. Give mannitol 0.5 g/kg and furosemide to force a diuresis
- E. Give sodium bicarbonate to a urine pH > 7.5

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** He has no urgent indication for RRT (refractory hyperkalaemia, severe acidosis, diuretic-resistant fluid overload, uraemic complications). AKIKI and STARRT-AKI showed that pre-emptive/accelerated RRT without such indications does not improve survival and increases RRT exposure, and a large proportion of patients in the delayed arms never needed RRT. Removing myoglobin with extracorporeal therapy has no proven outcome benefit; mannitol, diuretics and bicarbonate are unproven. Ref: Gaudry S et al. *N Engl J Med* 2016 (AKIKI); STARRT-AKI Investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W19-D1 · Diabetic emergencies

**1.** A 24-year-old woman with type 1 diabetes presents with vomiting, blood ketones 5.8 mmol/L, pH 7.12, bicarbonate 8 mmol/L, glucose 26 mmol/L and potassium 3.1 mmol/L. Regarding her treatment:

- A. Insulin should be withheld until potassium is ≥ 3.3–3.5 mmol/L while potassium is replaced. — T / F
- B. Potassium should be added to IV fluids once K is 3.5–5.5 mmol/L and urine output is adequate. — T / F
- C. Sodium bicarbonate is routinely indicated at a pH of 7.12. — T / F
- D. Her usual long-acting basal insulin should be continued alongside the IV insulin infusion. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.** Insulin drives potassium into cells; starting it with K < 3.3–3.5 mmol/L risks arrhythmia, so replace potassium first (2024 consensus). Add 40 mmol/L KCl to fluids when K is 3.5–5.5. Bicarbonate is not recommended unless pH < 7.0 (consensus) – it does not speed resolution and risks hypokalaemia and paradoxical CSF acidosis. Continuing basal insulin reduces rebound hyperglycaemia and ketosis when the IV infusion stops. Ref: Umpierrez GE et al. *Diabetes Care* 2024 (ADA/EASD/JBDS/AACE/DTS consensus).

</details>

**2.** A 31-year-old man is being treated for DKA with 0.9 % saline and a fixed-rate insulin infusion. Twelve hours later his chloride is 118 mmol/L. Regarding complications of DKA treatment:

- A. Hypokalaemia is a common complication of insulin therapy. — T / F
- B. Saline resuscitation typically produces a high-anion-gap acidosis. — T / F
- C. A rapid fall in plasma osmolality protects against cerebral oedema. — T / F
- D. Phosphate should be routinely replaced in all patients, even if asymptomatic. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.** Insulin and correction of acidosis shift potassium intracellularly – hypokalaemia is the commonest dangerous complication. Large-volume saline causes hyperchloraemic, normal-anion-gap acidosis, which can be mistaken for persisting ketoacidosis (check ketones); balanced crystalloids resolve DKA slightly faster. Rapid osmolality falls are a suspected contributor to cerebral oedema (mainly in children). Routine phosphate replacement has no proven benefit; replace only if severe (< 0.32 mmol/L) or with cardiac/respiratory compromise. Ref: Umpierrez GE et al. *Diabetes Care* 2024; Self WH et al. *JAMA Netw Open* 2020.

</details>

**3.** A 78-year-old man with type 2 diabetes presents after a week of confusion with glucose 52 mmol/L, Na 149 mmol/L, urea 30 mmol/L, blood ketones 0.8 mmol/L and pH 7.36. Regarding the hyperglycaemic hyperosmolar state (HHS):

- A. Diagnostic criteria include glucose ≥ 33.3 mmol/L (600 mg/dl). — T / F
- B. Effective (calculated) serum osmolality is typically > 320 mOsm/kg. — T / F
- C. A modest rise in serum sodium during initial saline resuscitation is acceptable provided osmolality is falling. — T / F
- D. A fixed-rate insulin infusion of 0.1 U/kg/h should be started at the same time as IV fluids in all patients. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.** HHS criteria (2024 consensus): glucose ≥ 33.3 mmol/L, effective osmolality > 300–320 mOsm/kg, no significant ketonaemia (< 3 mmol/L) and pH ≥ 7.3. Fluid alone lowers glucose; as glucose falls, sodium rises – acceptable if osmolality falls slowly (target 3–8 mOsm/kg/h). JBDS advises starting insulin only once glucose stops falling with fluids (at 0.05 U/kg/h), unless there is significant ketonaemia, because early insulin causes rapid osmotic shifts and hypovolaemia. Mixed DKA–HHS occurs in up to a third. Ref: Mustafa OG et al. *Diabet Med* 2023 (JBDS HHS); Umpierrez GE et al. *Diabetes Care* 2024.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W19-D2 · Glycaemic control in ICU

**1.** An ICU is revising its insulin protocol for ventilated adults. Regarding trials of glycaemic control in critical illness:

- A. NICE-SUGAR showed that a target of 4.5–6.0 mmol/L increased 90-day mortality compared with ≤ 10 mmol/L. — T / F
- B. The first Leuven trial (2001), which showed lower mortality with intensive insulin, was performed in medical ICU patients. — T / F
- C. TGC-fast (2023), in which early parenteral nutrition was withheld, showed lower mortality with tight glucose control. — T / F
- D. In NICE-SUGAR, moderate hypoglycaemia (2.3–3.9 mmol/L) was not associated with an increased risk of death. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.** NICE-SUGAR (6104 patients) found higher 90-day mortality with intensive control (27.5 % vs 24.9 %) and far more severe hypoglycaemia. Leuven I was a surgical (mostly cardiac) ICU trial with early PN; Leuven II (medical ICU) showed no overall mortality benefit. TGC-fast (9230 patients, no early PN) found no difference in ICU dependency or mortality. In a post hoc NICE-SUGAR analysis, both moderate and severe hypoglycaemia were associated with dose-dependent increases in mortality. Current targets are ~7.8–10 mmol/L. Ref: NICE-SUGAR Investigators. *N Engl J Med* 2009 and 2012; Gunst J et al. *N Engl J Med* 2023.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W19-D3 · Thyroid emergencies

**1.** A 42-year-old woman with Graves' disease stopped carbimazole 2 months ago. She presents with temperature 40.1 °C, AF at 165/min, heart failure, agitation, vomiting and diarrhoea; Burch–Wartofsky score is 70. Free T4 is markedly raised and TSH suppressed, and no infection or other precipitant is identified. All of the following are appropriate in her management EXCEPT:

- A. Aspirin as an antipyretic
- B. Propranolol (or esmolol if heart failure limits beta-blockade), titrated to heart rate
- C. A thionamide (propylthiouracil or methimazole)
- D. Hydrocortisone
- E. Iodine (e.g. Lugol's solution) given at least 1 hour after the thionamide

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Thyroid storm is treated by blocking synthesis (thionamide; PTU also inhibits peripheral T4→T3 conversion), blocking release (iodine, given ≥ 1 h after the thionamide so it is not used as substrate), beta-blockade (cautious, short-acting in heart failure), glucocorticoids, cooling and treating the precipitant; cholestyramine and plasma exchange are adjuncts. Salicylates displace T4/T3 from binding proteins and raise free hormone levels – use paracetamol and active cooling instead. Ref: Ross DS et al. *Thyroid* 2016 (ATA guidelines); Akamizu T et al. *Thyroid* 2012.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W19-D5 · Polyuria and diabetes insipidus

**1.** A 58-year-old woman who has taken lithium for 20 years for bipolar disorder is nil by mouth after bowel surgery. On day 2 she passes 5 L of urine a day, Na is 158 mmol/L, plasma osmolality 322 mOsm/kg and urine osmolality 150 mOsm/kg. After 2 µg of IV desmopressin, urine osmolality rises only to 175 mOsm/kg. Glucose, calcium and potassium are normal. Baseline copeptin is markedly raised. Which is the most likely diagnosis?

- A. Nephrogenic diabetes insipidus (arginine vasopressin resistance)
- B. Central diabetes insipidus (arginine vasopressin deficiency)
- C. Primary polydipsia
- D. Osmotic diuresis
- E. Cerebral salt wasting

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** Hypernatraemia with inappropriately dilute urine that fails to concentrate (< 50 % rise) after desmopressin, together with a high copeptin (> 21 pmol/L without prior stimulation), indicates renal resistance to vasopressin – classically lithium-induced. Central DI responds to desmopressin and has a low copeptin; primary polydipsia does not cause hypernatraemia; osmotic diuresis would have urine osmolality ≈ 300 or higher; cerebral salt wasting causes hyponatraemia. The 2022 nomenclature renames these AVP resistance and AVP deficiency. Ref: Fenske W et al. *N Engl J Med* 2018; Arima H et al. *Endocr J* 2022.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D1 · Upper gastrointestinal bleeding

**1.** A 76-year-old man taking apixaban and aspirin presents with haematemesis, HR 115/min and BP 90/55 mmHg. Platelets are 210 × 10⁹/L, fibrinogen 1.2 g/L after 6 units of red cells. Regarding haemostatic support:

- A. Four-factor PCC (or andexanet alfa where available) may be used for life-threatening bleeding on a factor Xa inhibitor. — T / F
- B. Platelet transfusion is recommended because he takes aspirin, despite a normal count. — T / F
- C. Intravenous vitamin K alone would reverse his anticoagulant effect within 1 hour. — T / F
- D. Fibrinogen replacement is indicated with a level of 1.2 g/L during major haemorrhage. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.** Andexanet or 4F-PCC (25–50 IU/kg) is suggested for life-threatening FXa-inhibitor bleeding. Routine platelet transfusion for antiplatelet-associated GI bleeding with a normal count is not recommended (no benefit, possible harm). Vitamin K has no effect on DOACs and, for warfarin, takes 6–24 h – PCC is needed for rapid reversal. In major bleeding, fibrinogen < 1.5 g/L should be replaced with fibrinogen concentrate or cryoprecipitate. Ref: Gralnek IM et al. *Endoscopy* 2021 (ESGE); Rossaint R et al. *Crit Care* 2023.

</details>

**2.** A 52-year-old man with Child–Pugh C cirrhosis has massive variceal bleeding that persists after terlipressin and two attempts at band ligation. Regarding rescue therapy:

- A. A self-expanding oesophageal metal stent is an alternative to balloon tamponade as a bridge to definitive therapy. — T / F
- B. Pre-emptive TIPS is indicated in Child–Pugh A patients with variceal bleeding. — T / F
- C. Balloon tamponade can safely remain inflated for up to 72 hours. — T / F
- D. Aspiration pneumonia and oesophageal rupture are recognised complications of balloon tamponade. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.** Baveno VII: in refractory oesophageal variceal bleeding, balloon tamponade or (preferably, if available) a self-expanding metal stent can bridge to rescue TIPS; stents caused fewer adverse events in an RCT. Pre-emptive TIPS within 72 h is for high-risk patients (Child C 10–13, or Child B > 7 with active bleeding at endoscopy). Tamponade should be limited to ~24 h because of pressure necrosis. Ref: de Franchis R et al. *J Hepatol* 2022 (Baveno VII); Escorsell À et al. *Hepatology* 2016.

</details>

**3.** A 67-year-old woman presents with melaena and a Glasgow-Blatchford score of 12. Regarding evidence-based management of upper GI bleeding:

- A. Tranexamic acid did not reduce death from bleeding and increased venous thromboembolic events in HALT-IT. — T / F
- B. A Glasgow-Blatchford score of 0–1 identifies patients who may be managed as outpatients. — T / F
- C. Urgent endoscopy within 6 h reduced mortality compared with endoscopy at 6–24 h in high-risk patients. — T / F
- D. Pre-endoscopy IV erythromycin should be avoided because it increases rebleeding. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.** HALT-IT (12 009 patients) found no benefit of high-dose TXA and more VTE. ESGE suggests GBS ≤ 1 as a threshold for outpatient management. Lau et al. (2020) found no mortality benefit of endoscopy within 6 h versus 6–24 h after resuscitation in high-risk patients. Erythromycin 250 mg IV 30–120 min before endoscopy improves mucosal visualisation and reduces the need for second-look endoscopy. Ref: HALT-IT Trial Collaborators. *Lancet* 2020; Lau JYW et al. *N Engl J Med* 2020; Gralnek IM et al. *Endoscopy* 2021.

</details>

**4.** A Minnesota/Sengstaken-type tube is being inserted in a ventilated patient with uncontrolled variceal bleeding. Regarding its safe use:

- A. The position of the gastric balloon in the stomach should be confirmed (e.g. by radiograph) before it is fully inflated. — T / F
- B. The oesophageal balloon should be inflated to about 80–100 mmHg. — T / F
- C. The oesophageal balloon is inflated first, before the gastric balloon. — T / F
- D. Endotracheal intubation before insertion is advisable in patients with massive haematemesis or impaired consciousness. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – T.** Inflation of the gastric balloon in the oesophagus causes rupture, so position must be confirmed (partial inflation and X-ray) before full inflation and gentle traction. The gastric balloon alone often controls bleeding; the oesophageal balloon is inflated only if bleeding persists, to ~30–40 mmHg, and deflated periodically. Airway protection is recommended because of aspiration risk. Ref: de Franchis R et al. *J Hepatol* 2022 (Baveno VII); Tripathi D et al. *Gut* 2015 (BSG guidelines).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D2 · Acute pancreatitis

**1.** A 45-year-old man presents with severe epigastric pain radiating to the back. Regarding the diagnosis and classification of acute pancreatitis (revised Atlanta 2012):

- A. Diagnosis requires two of three: typical pain, lipase or amylase > 3 × ULN, characteristic imaging findings. — T / F
- B. Moderately severe pancreatitis includes transient organ failure resolving within 48 h. — T / F
- C. Organ failure is defined as a modified Marshall score ≥ 2 in the respiratory, cardiovascular or renal system. — T / F
- D. The magnitude of the amylase rise correlates with disease severity. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.** Revised Atlanta: mild = no organ failure or local complications; moderately severe = transient organ failure (< 48 h) and/or local or systemic complications; severe = persistent organ failure > 48 h. Organ failure = modified Marshall score ≥ 2 in respiratory, cardiovascular or renal systems. Enzyme levels support the diagnosis but do not predict severity. Ref: Banks PA et al. *Gut* 2013.

</details>

**2.** A 56-year-old woman with gallstone pancreatitis develops persistent respiratory and renal failure. Regarding management of severe acute pancreatitis:

- A. Aggressive early fluid resuscitation increased fluid overload without improving outcomes compared with moderate fluid therapy. — T / F
- B. For infected necrosis, a step-up approach starting with percutaneous or endoscopic drainage is preferred, ideally delaying intervention until walled-off necrosis has formed (~4 weeks). — T / F
- C. Urgent ERCP within 24–72 h improves outcome in predicted severe biliary pancreatitis without cholangitis. — T / F
- D. Nasogastric feeding is a safe alternative to nasojejunal feeding. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.** WATERFALL (2022) was stopped for harm: aggressive Ringer's lactate caused more fluid overload with no benefit. PANTER and TENSION established step-up drainage over primary necrosectomy; POINTER found no advantage of immediate drainage over postponed. APEC (2020) showed no benefit of urgent ERCP in predicted severe biliary pancreatitis without cholangitis. Early enteral nutrition is preferred to PN, and nasogastric feeding is non-inferior to nasojejunal. Ref: de-Madaria E et al. *N Engl J Med* 2022; van Santvoort HC et al. *N Engl J Med* 2010; Schepers NJ et al. *Lancet* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D3 · Acute liver failure

**1.** A 24-year-old woman is admitted 50 hours after a staggered paracetamol overdose. She has grade 1 encephalopathy, INR 4.8, platelets 140 × 10⁹/L, lactate 2.4 mmol/L, pH 7.36 and creatinine 110 µmol/L. She is not bleeding. A central venous catheter is needed for NAC and fluids. Which is the most appropriate approach to her coagulopathy?

- A. Insert the line under ultrasound guidance without prophylactic correction of the INR
- B. Give fresh frozen plasma 15 ml/kg before line insertion
- C. Give recombinant factor VIIa before line insertion
- D. Give four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate to normalise the INR
- E. Give two pools of platelets before line insertion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** In ALF the INR overestimates bleeding risk: pro- and anticoagulant factors are both reduced and global haemostasis (viscoelastic testing) is usually rebalanced. Prophylactic correction is not recommended; it adds volume, may precipitate thrombosis and removes the INR as a key prognostic marker for transplant listing. Procedures under ultrasound guidance are safe; correct only for active bleeding or high-risk procedures (e.g. ICP bolt). Vitamin K once IV is reasonable to exclude deficiency. Ref: Wendon J et al. *J Hepatol* 2017 (EASL ALF guideline); Stravitz RT et al. *J Hepatol* 2012.

</details>

**2.** A 31-year-old man with hyperacute paracetamol-induced liver failure is intubated for grade 4 encephalopathy on day 3. Arterial ammonia is 210 µmol/L, Na 134 mmol/L, temperature 37.4 °C, MAP 80 mmHg on noradrenaline, and his pupils have become sluggish. He is listed for super-urgent transplantation. Which measure is most appropriate to reduce his risk of intracranial hypertension?

- A. Oral lactulose titrated to two to three stools per day
- B. Hypertonic saline to a serum sodium of 145–150 mmol/L
- C. Prophylactic hyperventilation to PaCO2 3.5 kPa
- D. Dexamethasone 8 mg IV 8-hourly
- E. Prophylactic hypothermia to 32 °C

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** Ammonia > 150–200 µmol/L, grade 3–4 encephalopathy, young age and vasopressor need predict cerebral oedema in hyperacute ALF. Hypertonic saline to Na 145–150 mmol/L reduced the incidence and severity of intracranial hypertension in an RCT. Early CRRT also lowers ammonia. Lactulose is ineffective and causes distension; prolonged hyperventilation is reserved for acute surges; steroids are ineffective; a trial of moderate hypothermia (33–34 °C) showed no benefit. Ref: Murphy N et al. *Hepatology* 2004; Bernal W et al. *J Hepatol* 2016; Wendon J et al. *J Hepatol* 2017.

</details>

**3.** Regarding the definition and classification of acute liver failure (ALF):

- A. In the O'Grady classification, hyperacute ALF is encephalopathy within 7 days of the onset of jaundice. — T / F
- B. Patients with acute presentation of Wilson's disease are excluded from the definition of ALF. — T / F
- C. Subacute ALF has a better prognosis without transplantation than hyperacute ALF. — T / F
- D. Encephalopathy is not required for the diagnosis of ALF. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – F, D – F.** ALF = coagulopathy (INR ≥ 1.5) and any degree of encephalopathy in a patient without pre-existing liver disease, within 26 weeks; acute severe liver injury without encephalopathy is a separate entity. Wilson's disease, vertically acquired HBV and autoimmune hepatitis are included despite possible pre-existing disease. Hyperacute (< 7 days, typically paracetamol) carries the highest risk of cerebral oedema but the best spontaneous survival; subacute (5–26 weeks) has less cerebral oedema but worse transplant-free survival. Ref: O'Grady JG et al. *Lancet* 1993; Wendon J et al. *J Hepatol* 2017.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D4 · Cirrhosis, hepatorenal syndrome and ACLF

**1.** A 61-year-old man with alcohol-related cirrhosis and tense ascites has a creatinine rising from 90 to 210 µmol/L over 4 days. Regarding the diagnosis of hepatorenal syndrome-AKI (HRS-AKI, ICA–ADQI criteria):

- A. Diagnosis requires no improvement in renal function after 48 h of diuretic withdrawal and albumin 1 g/kg. — T / F
- B. Shock and recent nephrotoxic drug exposure must be absent. — T / F
- C. Microhaematuria > 50 red cells per high-power field is compatible with the diagnosis. — T / F
- D. A urine sodium > 40 mmol/L supports the diagnosis. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.** HRS-AKI requires cirrhosis with ascites, KDIGO-defined AKI, no response to 2 days of diuretic withdrawal and albumin volume expansion, absence of shock and nephrotoxins, and no signs of structural kidney injury (proteinuria > 500 mg/day, microhaematuria > 50 RBC/hpf, abnormal ultrasound). Intense renal vasoconstriction produces avid sodium retention (UNa usually < 10–20 mmol/L, FeNa < 0.2 %). The 2024 ICA–ADQI update replaced types 1/2 with HRS-AKI and HRS-NAKI. Ref: Nadim MK et al. *J Hepatol* 2024; Angeli P et al. *J Hepatol* 2015.

</details>

**2.** A 58-year-old woman with alcohol-related cirrhosis and refractory ascites develops AKI (creatinine 95 → 240 µmol/L). After diuretic withdrawal and 2 days of albumin there is no improvement, urine sodium is 6 mmol/L, urine sediment is bland and ultrasound is normal, so she fulfils the criteria for HRS-AKI. Her SpO2 is 97 % on air and there is no evidence of volume overload. All of the following are appropriate EXCEPT:

- A. Terlipressin together with albumin
- B. Noradrenaline with albumin in the ICU if terlipressin cannot be used
- C. Assessment of oxygenation and volume status before and during terlipressin
- D. Furosemide to restore urine output
- E. Referral for liver transplant assessment

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** HRS-AKI is treated with a vasoconstrictor plus albumin (20–40 g/day): terlipressin (bolus or, increasingly, continuous infusion with fewer adverse effects) or noradrenaline in an ICU setting. CONFIRM (2021) showed more HRS reversal with terlipressin but more respiratory failure, so oxygenation and volume must be monitored (avoid if SpO2 < 90 % or ACLF grade 3 with high creatinine). Transplantation is the definitive treatment. Diuretics are stopped – they worsen renal hypoperfusion. Ref: Wong F et al. *N Engl J Med* 2021 (CONFIRM); EASL. *J Hepatol* 2018.

</details>

**3.** A 54-year-old man with cirrhosis is ventilated after a variceal bleed that has been controlled. Tense ascites is limiting diaphragmatic excursion and causing high airway pressures (plateau 32 cmH2O) and failure of a spontaneous breathing trial. He received terlipressin for the bleed and is on no diuretics. Under ultrasound guidance, large-volume paracentesis drains 7 L of ascitic fluid. Which is the most appropriate plasma expansion?

- A. No plasma expansion is required
- B. About 56 g of albumin (e.g. 280 ml of 20 % albumin)
- C. 20 g of albumin regardless of volume removed
- D. 1 L of 4.5 % albumin given over 24 h
- E. 500 ml of 6 % hydroxyethyl starch

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** EASL recommends 8 g of albumin per litre removed for paracentesis > 5 L to prevent post-paracentesis circulatory dysfunction (rising renin, hyponatraemia, AKI and mortality). 7 L × 8 g = 56 g ≈ 280 ml of 20 % albumin. Synthetic colloids are less effective and HES is associated with AKI; dilute albumin delivers too much volume and sodium. Ref: EASL. *J Hepatol* 2018 (decompensated cirrhosis guideline); Bernardi M et al. *Hepatology* 2012.

</details>

**4.** A 49-year-old woman with alcohol-related cirrhosis is admitted with pneumonia and develops respiratory failure requiring ventilation, AKI (creatinine 260 µmol/L) and noradrenaline-dependent shock. Bilirubin is 210 µmol/L and INR 2.4. On day 2 she remains on vasopressors and ventilated. The family asks about her chances of survival. Which tool is the most appropriate for estimating her short-term mortality?

- A. CLIF-C ACLF score
- B. Child–Pugh score
- C. Maddrey discriminant function
- D. Glasgow alcoholic hepatitis score
- E. MELD score alone

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A.** She has acute-on-chronic liver failure with ≥ 3 organ failures (ACLF grade 3). The CLIF-C ACLF score (CLIF-C OF score + age + WBC) predicts 28- and 90-day mortality more accurately than Child–Pugh or MELD; reassessment at days 3–7 refines prognosis and guides decisions about transplant or futility. Maddrey and Glasgow scores guide corticosteroid use in alcoholic hepatitis. Ref: Jalan R et al. *J Hepatol* 2014; EASL. *J Hepatol* 2023 (ACLF guideline).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W20-D5 · Intra-abdominal hypertension

**1.** A 63-year-old man is in ICU 18 h after an open repair of an incarcerated incisional hernia with small-bowel resection. He has received 7 L of fluid. Intravesical pressure measured correctly is 18 mmHg on two occasions, urine output is 1 ml/kg/h, creatinine is unchanged, MAP 80 mmHg and lactate 1.6 mmol/L. Which is the most appropriate management?

- A. Immediate decompressive laparotomy
- B. Nasogastric and rectal decompression, adequate analgesia-sedation, avoidance of positive fluid balance and serial IAP measurement
- C. Further fluid bolus to increase abdominal perfusion pressure
- D. Prophylactic continuous neuromuscular blockade for 48 h
- E. Start CRRT to remove fluid

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B.** IAP 18 mmHg = grade II intra-abdominal hypertension (12–15 grade I, 16–20 grade II, 21–25 grade III, > 25 grade IV). Without new organ dysfunction this is not abdominal compartment syndrome (sustained IAP > 20 mmHg with new organ failure), so medical measures are first: evacuate intraluminal contents, improve wall compliance (analgesia, sedation, avoid head-up > 30°, brief NMB only if needed), drain ascites if present, avoid fluid overload. Decompressive laparotomy is reserved for overt ACS. Ref: Kirkpatrick AW et al. *Intensive Care Med* 2013 (WSACS).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W21-D1 · Nutrition in critical illness

**1.** A 59-year-old woman with prolonged ileus after a Whipple procedure needs parenteral nutrition (PN). She was severely malnourished before surgery. Regarding PN:

- A. Peripheral PN is limited to solutions with an osmolarity of about 850–900 mOsm/L or less. — T / F
- B. In refeeding hypophosphataemia, temporary caloric restriction improved survival compared with standard feeding. — T / F
- C. ESPEN recommends full-target PN from day 1 in well-nourished ICU patients who cannot be fed enterally. — T / F
- D. Lipid emulsion is contraindicated in patients receiving a propofol infusion. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.** Peripheral veins tolerate only low-osmolarity solutions (< ~850–900 mOsm/L), otherwise thrombophlebitis occurs. In Doig's RCT, caloric restriction for 48 h when refeeding hypophosphataemia developed improved survival. ESPEN advises against early full feeding; when EN is contraindicated, PN should be introduced progressively (hypocaloric < 70 % of energy expenditure early), and in EPaNIC late PN (after day 8) was superior to early PN. Propofol lipid (1.1 kcal/ml) must be counted, and lipid dose adjusted, but it is not a contraindication. Ref: Singer P et al. *Clin Nutr* 2019 and 2023 (ESPEN); Doig GS et al. *Lancet Respir Med* 2015; Casaer MP et al. *N Engl J Med* 2011.

</details>

**2.** An 80-kg man on day 4 of ventilation for ARDS, with no previous malnutrition, is receiving propofol 1 % at 20 ml/h and enteral feed providing 1400 kcal/day. His estimated energy target is 1800 kcal/day and his triglycerides are rising (3.8 mmol/L). The dietitian asks whether non-nutritional calories are being counted. Approximately how many kilocalories per day does the propofol contribute?

- A. Negligible – propofol contains no calories
- B. About 100 kcal/day
- C. About 250 kcal/day
- D. About 1000 kcal/day
- E. About 530 kcal/day

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**E.** Propofol 1 % in 10 % lipid emulsion provides ~1.1 kcal/ml: 20 ml/h × 24 h = 480 ml × 1.1 ≈ 530 kcal/day. Together with his feed (1400 kcal) he receives ~1930 kcal – above target. ESPEN recommends counting non-nutritional calories (propofol, glucose infusions, citrate) to avoid overfeeding, and monitoring triglycerides (propofol infusion syndrome and pancreatitis risk). Reducing the feed volume would also lower protein, so a high-protein formula may be needed. Ref: Singer P et al. *Clin Nutr* 2019 (ESPEN); Bousie E et al. *Clin Nutr* 2016.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W21-D3 · Stress ulcer prophylaxis

**1.** A 68-year-old woman is ventilated for faecal peritonitis. She is on noradrenaline 0.3 µg/kg/min, platelets 40 × 10⁹/L and INR 2.1. Nasogastric feeding has been established at the full target rate. She has no history of peptic ulcer and takes no antiplatelet drugs. What is the most appropriate stress ulcer prophylaxis?

- A. None, because she is receiving full enteral nutrition
- B. Sucralfate via nasogastric tube
- C. A proton pump inhibitor (or H2-receptor antagonist)
- D. Misoprostol
- E. An alginate–antacid preparation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C.** Shock and coagulopathy are major risk factors for clinically important GI bleeding. The 2024 SCCM/ASHP guideline recommends PPI or H2RA prophylaxis for patients with these risk factors, irrespective of enteral feeding; REVISE (2024, 4821 ventilated patients) showed pantoprazole reduced clinically important upper GI bleeding without a mortality difference or significant increase in pneumonia or C. difficile. Sucralfate is less effective; alginates and misoprostol have no role. Stop prophylaxis once risk factors resolve. Ref: MacLaren R et al. *Crit Care Med* 2024; Cook D et al. *N Engl J Med* 2024 (REVISE).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W21-D5 · Clostridioides difficile infection

**1.** A 70-year-old man on a PPI develops profuse diarrhoea 10 days after a course of co-amoxiclav. Regarding C. difficile infection (CDI):

- A. Proton pump inhibitor use is associated with an increased risk of CDI. — T / F
- B. Ribotype 027 strains produce binary toxin in addition to toxins A and B. — T / F
- C. Alcohol-based hand rub reliably kills C. difficile spores. — T / F
- D. A positive nucleic acid amplification test (NAAT) alone may overdiagnose CDI because it detects colonisation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – T.** Acid suppression, antibiotics (clindamycin, cephalosporins, fluoroquinolones, co-amoxiclav), age and hospitalisation are risk factors. Hypervirulent ribotype 027 produces binary toxin and more toxin A/B. Spores resist alcohol – soap-and-water hand washing, contact precautions and sporicidal (chlorine) cleaning are needed. Two-step testing (GDH or NAAT followed by toxin EIA) distinguishes infection from colonisation. Ref: McDonald LC et al. *Clin Infect Dis* 2018 (IDSA/SHEA); Crobach MJT et al. *Clin Microbiol Infect* 2016 (ESCMID).

</details>

**2.** A 74-year-old woman with C. difficile infection becomes hypotensive (MAP 58 mmHg on noradrenaline), with abdominal distension, absent bowel sounds and lactate 4.5 mmol/L. CT shows a dilated colon (8 cm) without perforation. She has an ileus and only small amounts of stool. Which is the most appropriate antimicrobial regimen?

- A. Oral metronidazole 400 mg three times daily
- B. Oral fidaxomicin 200 mg twice daily alone
- C. IV vancomycin 1 g twice daily
- D. Oral (± rectal) vancomycin 500 mg four times daily plus IV metronidazole
- E. Faecal microbiota transplantation as first-line therapy

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D.** Fulminant CDI (hypotension, ileus, megacolon) is treated with high-dose enteral vancomycin – via NG tube and, with ileus, as a retention enema – plus IV metronidazole (which reaches the colon via biliary excretion and inflamed mucosa), with early surgical review for colectomy or loop ileostomy with lavage. IV vancomycin does not reach the colonic lumen. Fidaxomicin is first-line for non-fulminant episodes but has limited data in fulminant disease; FMT is used for recurrences or refractory cases. Ref: Johnson S et al. *Clin Infect Dis* 2021 (IDSA/SHEA update); van Prehn J et al. *Clin Microbiol Infect* 2021 (ESCMID).

</details>


## Case-style practice · W22-D1 · Red cell transfusion

**1.** An ICU is updating its red-cell transfusion policy for several patient groups. Regarding randomised evidence on transfusion thresholds:

- A. In septic shock (TRISS), a threshold of 70 g/L resulted in similar 90-day mortality to 90 g/L with fewer transfusions. — T / F
- B. In acute myocardial infarction with anaemia (MINT), a liberal strategy (100 g/L) significantly reduced death or reinfarction at 30 days. — T / F
- C. In patients with acute brain injury (TRAIN), a liberal strategy reduced the proportion with unfavourable neurological outcome. — T / F
- D. In cardiac surgery (TRICS-III), a restrictive threshold of 75 g/L was inferior to a liberal one. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – F, C – T, D – F.** TRISS (n = 1005) showed no mortality difference and halved transfusion use. MINT (n = 3504) found a non-significant trend towards fewer events with a liberal strategy (16.9 % vs 14.5 %; RR 1.15, p = 0.07) – so a liberal approach is reasonable but not proven. TRAIN (2024) found fewer unfavourable 180-day outcomes with a 90 g/L threshold in TBI, SAH and ICH; HEMOTION (TBI) showed a non-significant trend in the same direction. TRICS-III showed non-inferiority of restrictive transfusion. Ref: Holst LB et al. *N Engl J Med* 2014; Carson JL et al. *N Engl J Med* 2023; Taccone FS et al. *JAMA* 2024; Mazer CD et al. *N Engl J Med* 2017.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W22-D2 · Blood products and coagulation disorders

**1.** A 46-year-old woman is bleeding heavily after a liver resection. Regarding blood components and haemostatic factors:

- A. Two pools of cryoprecipitate (10 donor units) raise the fibrinogen of an adult by roughly 1 g/L. — T / F
- B. Four-factor prothrombin complex concentrate contains factors II, VII, IX and X. — T / F
- C. Fibrinogen concentrate also contains clinically useful amounts of factor VIII and von Willebrand factor. — T / F
- D. Ionised hypocalcaemia has no clinically relevant effect on coagulation. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – F, D – F.** Cryoprecipitate contains fibrinogen, FVIII, FXIII, vWF and fibronectin; fibrinogen concentrate is purified fibrinogen (FXIII only in small amounts). 4F-PCC contains II, VII, IX, X (plus protein C/S); 3F-PCC lacks adequate FVII. Calcium is a cofactor for several clotting steps and hypocalcaemia (frequent with citrated products) independently predicts mortality in major haemorrhage – keep iCa > 1.1 mmol/L. Ref: Rossaint R et al. *Crit Care* 2023 (European trauma bleeding guideline); Stanworth SJ et al. *Br J Haematol* 2022 (BSH major haemorrhage).

</details>

**2.** A 66-year-old man on day 7 of unfractionated heparin after vascular surgery has a fall in platelets from 260 to 95 × 10⁹/L and a new DVT. Regarding acquired disorders of haemostasis:

- A. A low 4Ts score (≤ 3) has a high negative predictive value for heparin-induced thrombocytopenia (HIT). — T / F
- B. In strongly suspected HIT, heparin should be stopped and a non-heparin anticoagulant (e.g. argatroban) started before results are available. — T / F
- C. In acquired haemophilia A, a prolonged APTT does not correct after mixing with normal plasma. — T / F
- D. Warfarin should be started immediately when HIT is diagnosed. — T / F

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A – T, B – T, C – T, D – F.** The 4Ts (thrombocytopenia, timing, thrombosis, other causes) has an NPV > 99 % when low. With intermediate/high probability, stop all heparin (including flushes) and start a non-heparin anticoagulant (argatroban, bivalirudin, fondaparinux or a DOAC). An inhibitor (autoantibody to FVIII) prevents APTT correction on mixing, unlike factor deficiency. Warfarin started in acute HIT causes protein C depletion and venous limb gangrene – start only once platelets recover (> 150 × 10⁹/L) with overlap. Ref: Cuker A et al. *Blood Adv* 2018 (ASH HIT guideline); Tiede A et al. *Haematologica* 2020.

</details>


## Case-style practice · W22-D3 · Plasma exchange and TTP

**1.** A 39-year-old woman with myasthenic crisis is started on therapeutic plasma exchange (TPE). Regarding TPE:

- A. Exchanging 1–1.5 plasma volumes removes roughly 60–75 % of an intravascular substance. — T / F
- B. Sepsis is an ASFA category I indication. — T / F
- C. Highly protein-bound drugs with a small volume of distribution (e.g. IVIg, rituximab) are best given immediately before a TPE session. — T / F
